} .tour__bar { gap: .4rem; } .hudbox { font-size: .6rem; } } </style> <div class="wrap" id="vj"> <header class="head"> <div class="plate">Teardown · Latent world models · rev. 2026-09-22</div> <h1>V-JEPA 2 and 2-AC, taken apart</h1> <p class="lede"> V-JEPA 2 learns a video encoder by predicting masked video in a feature space it invents. V-JEPA 2-AC freezes that encoder, trains an action-conditioned predictor on under 62 hours of robot video, and plans with it on Franka arms it never saw. This page checks every number against the paper, the released code and the successor, audits the robot evidence trial by trial, and ends in a sixteen-step 3D tour and a research agenda for manipulation and humanoids. </p> <div class="byline"> <span>Primary source: arXiv 2506.09985v1, 11 Jun 2025</span> <span>Code: facebookresearch/vjepa2 @ 204698b</span> <span>Successor: V-JEPA 2.1, arXiv 2603.14482v3</span> <span>Reading: 40 min, tour: 20 min</span> </div> <div class="jump"> <a class="primary" href="#tour">Open the 3D tour</a> <a href="#story">Story mode</a> <a href="#s4">V-JEPA 2-AC</a> <a href="#s5">Evaluation audit</a> <a href="#future">Physical AI directions</a> </div> <div class="filter" id="vj-filter"> <span class="filter__label">Provenance</span> <label class="sw sw--pap"><input type="checkbox" data-evf="pap" checked><span class="sw__dot"></span> Paper</label> <label class="sw sw--code"><input type="checkbox" data-evf="code" checked><span class="sw__dot"></span> Code</label> <label class="sw sw--ctx"><input type="checkbox" data-evf="ctx" checked><span class="sw__dot"></span> Context</label> <label class="sw sw--inf"><input type="checkbox" data-evf="inf" checked><span class="sw__dot"></span> Inference</label> <label class="sw sw--spec"><input type="checkbox" data-evf="spec" checked><span class="sw__dot"></span> Speculation</label> <span class="filter__note" id="vj-filter-note">All shown</span> </div> </header> <!-- 0 ------------------------------------------------------------------ --> <section id="s0"> <div class="sec-head"><span class="num">00</span><h2>Corrections before the answers</h2></div> <div class="col"> <p> Fourteen premises that circulate about these models, including three that the previous version of this page got wrong. Each is checked against the paper, the code at commit 204698b, or a named primary source. Tags: <span class="ev ev--pap">Paper</span> the V-JEPA 2 paper, <span class="ev ev--code">Code</span> the official repository or numbers measured by running it, <span class="ev ev--ctx">Context</span> another primary source, <span class="ev ev--inf">Inference</span> and <span class="ev ev--spec">Speculation</span> my own reasoning. </p> </div> <ol class="fixes"> <li data-ev="pap"><b>The name is V-JEPA 2-AC.</b> "V-JEPA-AC" does not appear in the paper or the code. <span class="ev ev--pap">Paper §3</span></li> <li data-ev="pap"><b>"Unlabeled" robot video still carries proprioception.</b> Every DROID frame comes with a 7-D end-effector state (3 position, 3 extrinsic Euler angles, 1 gripper), and each action is computed as the state change between frames. "Unlabeled" means no reward, no task label and no success flag; the 23k trajectories include failures. <span class="ev ev--pap">Paper §3.1, §4.1 fn. 1</span></li> <li data-ev="pap"><b>The deployed planner looks one step ahead.</b> Every robot number in Tables 2 and 3 plans a single action with 800 samples, the top 10 kept, 10 CEM iterations, then replans. Long-horizon structure comes from sub-goal images a human supplies, not from lookahead in the model. <span class="ev ev--pap">Paper App. B.2, Table 3</span></li> <li data-ev="pap"><b>The energy is a terminal distance.</b> It is the L1 distance between the predicted representation T steps ahead and the goal representation, not a cost summed along the trajectory. <span class="ev ev--pap">Paper Eq. 5</span></li> <li data-ev="pap"><b>V-JEPA 2 has no EMA schedule.</b> The teacher momentum is fixed at 0.99925 in both phases. The ramp from 0.998 to 1.0 belongs to V-JEPA 1, and a ramp from 0.999 to 1.0 to the paper's 90k-step "abbreviated" ablation recipe. The previous version of this page stated the V-JEPA 1 schedule for V-JEPA 2. <span class="ev ev--pap">Paper §2.4, Tables 9 and 10</span> <span class="ev ev--code">configs/train/vitg16</span></li> <li data-ev="code"><b>V-JEPA 2 states no masking ratio, and "about 90%" is not what its sampler does.</b> It reuses V-JEPA's multiblock recipe: a short-range mask (union of 8 blocks, each 15% of the frame) and a long-range mask (union of 2 blocks, each 70%), both spanning the whole clip in time. Running the released sampler on 16-frame 256 px clips: 62% and 81% of tokens masked per clip. The collator then truncates every clip to the batch minimum, so at 24 clips per GPU the encoder sees 25% and 6% of tokens, the predictor targets 47% and 70%, and the rest are neither seen nor predicted. V-JEPA 1 describes the same recipe as "an average masking ratio of ∼90%". The previous version of this page repeated that figure. <span class="ev ev--code">measured, src/masks/multiseq_multiblock3d.py</span> <span class="ev ev--ctx">V-JEPA §3.2</span></li> <li data-ev="pap"><b>The V-JEPA 2-AC encoder never sees a video clip.</b> Each frame is encoded on its own, duplicated to fill a 2-frame tubelet, giving a 16 × 16 × 1408 feature map. All temporal reasoning in the world model happens in the predictor. <span class="ev ev--pap">Paper §3.1</span> <span class="ev ev--code">app/vjepa_droid/train.py, forward_target</span></li> <li data-ev="pap"><b>The robot evidence is 10 trials per cell.</b> Two labs, one trained model, no seeds, no intervals. The Cosmos baseline ran in Lab 2 only. <span class="ev ev--pap">Paper Tables 2 and 3</span></li> <li data-ev="ctx"><b>IntPhys 2 and CausalVQA are not in the V-JEPA 2 paper.</b> MVP is (44.5 paired accuracy). IntPhys 2 reports V-JEPA 2 in its own companion paper; the CausalVQA paper has no V-JEPA 2 entry. <span class="ev ev--ctx">IntPhys 2 Table 2; CausalVQA arXiv 2506.09943</span></li> <li data-ev="pap"><b>The Epic-Kitchens "prediction" result is mostly encoder semantics.</b> A probe on encoder features alone reaches 39.1 recall@5; adding the predictor's output lifts it to 39.7; the predictor's output alone gives 20.2. <span class="ev ev--pap">Paper App. D.2, Table 20</span></li> <li data-ev="pap"><b>PerceptionTest 84.0 is not a zero-shot number.</b> It is the test-set accuracy after supervised fine-tuning on PerceptionTest; the other rows of the same table are zero-shot. <span class="ev ev--pap">Paper Table 8 caption</span></li> <li data-ev="inf"><b>"L1-ball of radius 0.075" cannot give the stated 13 cm step.</b> An L1 ball of that radius caps displacement at 7.5 cm. A per-axis box of half-width 0.075 caps it at 0.075·√3 ≈ 13 cm, which is the figure the paper gives, and the released planner clips each axis, which is a box. <span class="ev ev--pap">Paper §4.1, Fig. 8</span> <span class="ev ev--code">notebooks/utils/mpc_utils.py</span> <span class="ev ev--inf">Inference</span></li> <li data-ev="code"><b>The released code is not the paper's configuration.</b> The DROID config trains on 8-frame clips (7 teacher-forced steps), not 16-frame clips with T = 15. The released pretraining config mixes three datasets (K710, SSv2, HowTo100M), not VideoMix22M. The released planner samples 400 actions over a horizon of 2 with rotation frozen at zero, not 800 over a horizon of 1. <span class="ev ev--code">configs/train/vitg16/*.yaml, notebooks/utils/world_model_wrapper.py</span></li> <li data-ev="ctx"><b>A successor exists.</b> V-JEPA 2.1 (arXiv 2603.14482v3, 11 Jun 2026) adds a dense loss on visible tokens, deep self-supervision at intermediate layers, modality-specific tokenizers and a 2B ViT-G. With the same 300M action-conditioned predictor it reports Grasp rising from 60% to 70% at identical planner settings, and to 80% with an 8-step horizon. This page keeps V-JEPA 2 and 2-AC as the subject and flags 2.1 where it changes a conclusion. <span class="ev ev--ctx">V-JEPA 2.1 §3.3, Table 6</span></li> </ol> </section> <!-- 1 ------------------------------------------------------------------ --> <section id="s1"> <div class="sec-head"><span class="num">01</span><h2>Why predict in a space you chose</h2></div> <div class="col"> <h3>The energy view</h3> <p data-ev="ctx"> LeCun's 2022 position paper recasts prediction as an energy-based model: a scalar compatibility <span class="eq">E(x, y)</span> that is low when the continuation <span class="eq">y</span> fits the context <span class="eq">x</span> and high otherwise, with no requirement that it normalise into a density. A joint-embedding predictive architecture computes that energy in representation space. An encoder maps both sides, a predictor carries the context forward under a conditioning variable <span class="eq">z</span>, and a distance scores the match. <span class="ev ev--ctx">LeCun 2022, §4</span> </p> <div class="formula">E(x, y, z) = D( P<sub>φ</sub>(s<sub>x</sub>, z), s<sub>y</sub> ),   s<sub>x</sub> = E<sub>θ</sub>(x),  s<sub>y</sub> = sg(E<sub>θ̄</sub>(y)) <span class="dim">  // V-JEPA 2: z = positions of the masked tokens, D = L1</span></div> <p data-ev="inf"> The argument against pixel prediction is a decomposition. For any predictor ŷ of y given x, <span class="eq">E‖y − ŷ‖² = ‖E[y|x] − ŷ‖² + tr Cov(y|x)</span>. The second term is irreducible, and in natural video it is dominated by content nothing can forecast: foliage, water, sensor noise, specular highlights. A likelihood objective still hands out gradient in proportion to per-pixel error, so capacity drifts toward the nuisance. A JEPA pays only for what survives the encoder, and the encoder is free to drop what it cannot predict. The paper states the same intuition with "each blade of grass in a field". <span class="ev ev--pap">Paper §1</span> Littwin et al. (NeurIPS 2024) prove a matching statement for deep linear networks: latent prediction prioritises features with large regression coefficients, reconstruction prioritises high-variance ones. <span class="ev ev--ctx">Littwin et al. 2024</span> </p> <div class="note note--warn" data-ev="inf"> <p><b>The caveat the motivation glosses over.</b> A deterministic predictor trained with L1 converges to the coordinate-wise conditional median of the target features. Moving to latent space removes multimodality only if the encoder maps the distinct outcomes to nearby points, which means it has discarded the stochastic factor. In manipulation that factor is often the one that decides the task: does the cup slip or not. <span class="ev ev--inf">Inference</span></p> </div> <p>Step 1 of the tour turns the decomposition into a slider. <a class="goto" href="#tour" data-goto="1">open step 1 →</a></p> </div> <div class="panel"> <div class="panel__head">What each objective forces the network to model</div> <div class="panel__body tablewrap"> <table> <thead><tr><th scope="col">Family</th><th scope="col">Target</th><th scope="col">What it must represent</th><th scope="col">Frozen SSv2, same protocol</th><th scope="col">Cost to use as a simulator</th></tr></thead> <tbody> <tr><td>Pixel reconstruction (MAE, VideoMAEv2)</td><td style="white-space:normal">masked pixels</td><td style="white-space:normal">low-level statistics of every pixel, predictable or not</td><td>56.1 (VideoMAEv2 1B, literature row)</td><td style="white-space:normal">needs a decoder; not action-conditioned</td></tr> <tr><td>Image-text contrastive (SigLIP2, PE)</td><td style="white-space:normal">matching caption</td><td style="white-space:normal">what a caption names; invariant to what it omits, including most motion</td><td>49.9 / 55.4</td><td style="white-space:normal">no predictive model</td></tr> <tr><td>Image SSL (DINOv2)</td><td style="white-space:normal">teacher features of another crop</td><td style="white-space:normal">per-frame semantics and geometry</td><td>50.7</td><td style="white-space:normal">no predictive model</td></tr> <tr><td>Generative video world model (Cosmos, Sora, Genie)</td><td style="white-space:normal">future frames or their VAE latents</td><td style="white-space:normal">a full density over future appearance</td><td>not reported</td><td style="white-space:normal">Cosmos: 4 min per planned action on an RTX 4090</td></tr> <tr class="best"><td>JEPA (V-JEPA 2 ViT-g)</td><td style="white-space:normal">its own EMA features of the masked region</td><td style="white-space:normal">whatever about the region is predictable from its surroundings</td><td>75.3</td><td style="white-space:normal">16 s per planned action for 10× the samples</td></tr> </tbody> </table> </div> <div class="panel__foot" data-ev="pap">SSv2 top-1 with a frozen 4-layer attentive probe at 256 px, Table 4; the VideoMAEv2 figure is a literature row with a different probe. Planning times: Table 3. <span class="ev ev--pap">Paper Tables 3 and 4</span></div> </div> <div class="col"> <h3>The bet, and what would falsify it</h3> <p data-ev="pap"> The stated target is to "learn to understand the world and learn to act largely by observation". The recipe splits the learning in two: action-free pretraining on over a million hours of internet video, then action-conditioned post-training on under 62 hours of one robot's logs. <span class="ev ev--pap">Paper abstract, Fig. 1</span> </p> <p data-ev="inf"> The split is the central bet because interaction data is the scarce resource. If the representation learned from watching carries the state a controller needs, a small amount of interaction is enough to attach actions to it. Three results would falsify it. First, an action-conditioned predictor trained on the same 62 hours over an image encoder (DINOv2, as in DINO-WM) or a random one matching V-JEPA 2-AC's success would mean the video pretraining is not what carries the result; the paper runs no such encoder ablation for the AC stage. Second, success that scales with robot hours but not with pretraining scale. Third, a behaviour-cloning policy on the same 62 hours matching it. Octo, trained on all of DROID with hindsight goals, loses, which supports the bet but changes architecture, objective and pretraining at once. <span class="ev ev--inf">Inference</span> </p> </div> </section> <!-- 2 ------------------------------------------------------------------ --> <section id="s2"> <div class="sec-head"><span class="num">02</span><h2>V-JEPA 2: architecture and pretraining</h2></div> <div class="panel"> <div class="panel__head">Encoders and predictor</div> <div class="panel__body tablewrap"> <table> <thead><tr><th scope="col">Network</th><th scope="col">Params</th><th scope="col">Width</th><th scope="col">Depth</th><th scope="col">Heads</th><th scope="col">MLP</th><th scope="col">Embedder</th></tr></thead> <tbody> <tr><td>Encoder ViT-L</td><td>300M</td><td>1024</td><td>24</td><td>16</td><td>4096</td><td>2×16×16 strided conv</td></tr> <tr><td>Encoder ViT-H</td><td>600M</td><td>1280</td><td>32</td><td>16</td><td>5120</td><td>2×16×16 strided conv</td></tr> <tr class="best"><td>Encoder ViT-g</td><td>1B</td><td>1408</td><td>40</td><td>22</td><td>6144</td><td>2×16×16 strided conv</td></tr> <tr><td>Predictor (pretraining)</td><td>22M</td><td>384</td><td>12</td><td>12</td><td>1536</td><td>linear 1408 → 384</td></tr> <tr><td>Predictor (V-JEPA 2-AC)</td><td>∼300M</td><td>1024</td><td>24</td><td>16</td><td>4096</td><td>linear 1408 → 1024</td></tr> </tbody> </table> </div> <div class="panel__foot" data-ev="pap">Paper Table 12 and §3.1. The pretraining predictor is the same ViT-s for every encoder size. <span class="ev ev--pap">Paper App. A.3</span></div> </div> <div class="col"> <p data-ev="pap"> Position enters through a 3D rotary embedding: each head's feature dimension is split into three near-equal segments, rotated by the temporal, height and width index respectively. The paper reports it stabilised training of the largest models, relative to V-JEPA's absolute sin-cos table. <span class="ev ev--pap">Paper §2.1</span> In code each axis gets 2·⌊⌊d<sub>head</sub>/3⌋/2⌋ dimensions, so a 64-dim ViT-g head rotates 20 + 20 + 20 and leaves 4 untouched, and the 32-dim pretraining predictor head rotates 10 + 10 + 10 and leaves 2. <span class="ev ev--code">src/models/utils/modules.py, RoPEAttention</span> </p> <h3>One pretraining step, with shapes</h3> <p>ViT-g, a 16-frame 256 px clip, the short-range mask, B clips per GPU. |C| and |M| are the context and target counts after the collator's batch-minimum truncation, measured at B = 24.</p> </div> <div class="panel"> <div class="panel__body"> <pre class="code">x (B, 3, 16, 256, 256) <i># 4 fps, so 4 s of video</i> Conv3d k=s=(2,16,16) → (B, 1408, 8, 16, 16) → flatten → (B, 2048, 1408) <b>context</b> keep C (B, |C|, 1408) |C| ≈ 511 short-range, 129 long-range <b>encoder</b> E<sub>θ</sub>, 40 blocks, 3D-RoPE at each token's (t,h,w) → (B, |C|, 1408) <b>predictor</b> linear 1408→384 → (B, |C|, 384) ⊕ mask tokens Δ<sub>y</sub> at M → (B, |C|+|M|, 384) 12 blocks → keep M → linear 384→1408 → (B, |M|, 1408) |M| ≈ 953 / 1442 <b>target</b> E<sub>θ̄</sub> on all 2048 tokens, no grad → LayerNorm → select M → (B, |M|, 1408) <b>loss</b> mean |ẑ − z̄| over M and 1408 channels, averaged over the two masks</pre> </div> <div class="panel__foot" data-ev="code">Shapes from Table 9 and 12; LayerNorm on targets, the per-mask average and the counts from app/vjepa/train.py and the sampler, run at commit 204698b. <span class="ev ev--code">Code, measured</span></div> </div> <div class="col"> <h3>The objective and the teacher</h3> <div class="formula">min<sub>θ, φ, Δ<sub>y</sub></sub> ‖ P<sub>φ</sub>(Δ<sub>y</sub>, E<sub>θ</sub>(x)) − sg(E<sub>θ̄</sub>(y)) ‖<sub>1</sub>,    θ̄ ← m·θ̄ + (1 − m)·θ,  m = 0.99925</div> <p data-ev="pap"> Eq. 1 of the paper. Δ<sub>y</sub> is a learnable mask token placed at each dropped position, the loss is taken only over masked positions, and the teacher weights θ̄ are an exponential moving average of the student's. A fixed m of 0.99925 averages over about 1/(1 − m) ≈ 1,333 steps. <span class="ev ev--pap">Paper Eq. 1, Table 9</span> </p> <h3>Why it does not collapse</h3> <p data-ev="ctx"> The prediction term alone is minimised by a constant encoder, so every JEPA needs a mechanism that keeps the representation from going flat. V-JEPA 2 relies on three: a stop-gradient on the target branch, so the target is never pulled toward the prediction; an EMA teacher, a lagged and non-degenerate copy; and an asymmetric predictor, which gives the online branch a degree of freedom the target lacks. Tian, Chen and Ganguli (ICML 2021) show for linear networks that the predictor comes to share eigenvectors with the representation's correlation matrix and that eigenmodes above a threshold grow rather than decay. SimSiam (Chen and He, CVPR 2021) shows stop-gradient plus a predictor suffice without an EMA. None of this is a guarantee: partial dimensional collapse is common in non-contrastive methods. <span class="ev ev--ctx">Tian et al. 2021; Chen and He 2021</span> </p> <p data-ev="pap"> What V-JEPA 2 shows about collapse empirically: nothing. The paper asserts that the stop-gradient and EMA "prevent representation collapse" and reports no rank, spectrum or variance diagnostic. <span class="ev ev--pap">Paper §2.1</span> </p> <p data-ev="ctx"> The contrast is with methods that regularise the batch directly. VICReg hinges each dimension's standard deviation above a floor and penalises off-diagonal covariance. SIGReg, used by LeWM, constrains the batch to an isotropic Gaussian by testing 1-D projections against the normal characteristic function (M = 1024 projections, weight 0.1). It removes the EMA, the stop-gradient and the pretrained encoder and trains a 15M-parameter world model from pixels, at a price: every dimension must carry unit variance. On the 2-D Two-Room task LeWM reaches 87% where the other planners reach 97% to 100%, which its authors attribute to SIGReg in very low-complexity environments. <span class="ev ev--ctx">LeWM arXiv 2603.19312, §3 and Fig. 6</span> </p> <p>Step 5 runs a toy linear JEPA live and lets you remove each mechanism. <a class="goto" href="#tour" data-goto="5">open step 5 →</a></p> <h3>Masking, measured</h3> <p data-ev="code"> Both masks are unions of rectangular blocks that span the full clip in time, so each is a set of tubes. Block size is drawn once per batch (spatial scale 0.15 or 0.7 of the frame, aspect ratio in [0.75, 1.5]) and block positions per clip. The collator then truncates every clip's context and target index lists to the batch minimum. The truncation keeps the first indices in (t, h, w) order, so a heavily masked clip loses its last time slices from the target, and a lightly masked clip loses its last visible time slices from the context. <span class="ev ev--code">src/masks/multiseq_multiblock3d.py</span> </p> </div> <div class="panel"> <div class="panel__head">The released sampler, run 200 batches per setting</div> <div class="panel__body tablewrap"> <table> <thead><tr><th scope="col">Setting</th><th scope="col">Tokens</th><th scope="col">Masked per clip</th><th scope="col">Encoder sees</th><th scope="col">Predicted</th><th scope="col">Neither</th></tr></thead> <tbody> <tr><td>Pretrain 16 f × 256², short-range, B = 24</td><td>2,048</td><td>62%</td><td>511 (25%)</td><td>953 (47%)</td><td>28%</td></tr> <tr><td>Pretrain 16 f × 256², long-range, B = 24</td><td>2,048</td><td>81%</td><td>129 (6%)</td><td>1,442 (70%)</td><td>23%</td></tr> <tr><td>Cooldown 64 f × 384², short-range, B = 6</td><td>18,432</td><td>61%</td><td>5,534 (30%)</td><td>9,585 (52%)</td><td>18%</td></tr> <tr><td>Cooldown 64 f × 384², long-range, B = 6</td><td>18,432</td><td>81%</td><td>2,075 (11%)</td><td>13,533 (73%)</td><td>15%</td></tr> </tbody> </table> </div> <div class="panel__foot" data-ev="code">Measured by calling the repository's _MaskGenerator with the Table 9 parameters and the per-GPU batch sizes of the released configs; "masked per clip" is the untruncated union, from a numpy port that matches the real sampler to three decimals. <span class="ev ev--code">Code, measured</span></div> </div> <div class="col"> <p data-ev="ctx"> Why tubes: adjacent frames are nearly redundant, so a masked token with a visible copy at t ± 1 can be interpolated rather than inferred. V-JEPA's ablation found multiblock beats random-tube masking at 90%, which produced "features of low semantic quality", and beats causal masking from early frames only. <span class="ev ev--ctx">V-JEPA §4</span> Because the context contains frames on both sides of every masked step, this is spatiotemporal inpainting, not extrapolation: V-JEPA 2 pretraining produces a state encoder, and the explicit predict-the-next-frame objective appears only in V-JEPA 2-AC. <span class="ev ev--inf">Inference</span> <a class="goto" href="#tour" data-goto="3">open step 3 →</a> </p> <h3>The scaling recipe, Each ingredient is measured on top of the previous one, with one seed, so the order matters and there are no intervals. <span class="ev ev--pap">Paper §2.2 to §2.4</span> <span class="ev ev--inf">Inference on order dependence</span></div> </div> <div class="col"> <p data-ev="pap"> <b>Data.</b> VideoMix22M samples SSv2 (168k clips, 168 h, weight 0.056), Kinetics 400/600/700 (733k, 614 h, 0.188), HowTo100M (1.1M, 134k h, 0.318), curated YT-Temporal-1B (19M, 1.6M h, 0.188) and ImageNet (1M images, repeated into 16 frames, 0.250). Curation splits YT1B into 316M scenes with PySceneDetect, embeds each scene's middle frame with DINOv2 ViT-L, clusters into 1.5M clusters, keeps the 210k clusters that contain at least one Kinetics, SSv2, COIN or Epic-Kitchens training video, and reweights them toward that target mix, leaving 115M scenes. <span class="ev ev--pap">Paper Table 1, App. A.2, Table 11</span> Appendix A.2 gives YT1B as 1.4M hours and Table 1 as 1.6M. <span class="ev ev--pap">Paper</span> </p> <p data-ev="pap"> <b>Schedule and resolution.</b> A 12k-iteration warmup and a 228k-iteration constant phase at 16 frames and 256 px, then a 12k-iteration cooldown at 64 frames and 384 px with the learning rate decayed from 5.25e-4 to 1e-6, at a global batch of 3,072. Training at 64 × 384 × 384 throughout "would require roughly 60 GPU-years"; the progressive schedule cuts GPU time 8.4×. <span class="ev ev--pap">Paper §2.4, Table 9, Fig. 5</span> </p> <p data-ev="inf"> <b>Token counts.</b> A pretraining clip is (16/2)·(256/16)² = 8 · 256 = <b>2,048</b> tokens. A cooldown clip is (64/2)·(384/16)² = 32 · 576 = <b>18,432</b> tokens, 9× more, so the attention term of each layer costs about 81× more. A V-JEPA 2-AC frame is 1 · 256 = <b>256</b> tokens, and the VidQA model at 384 px feeds the LLM 576 tokens per 2-frame tubelet, the 288 per frame the paper quotes. <span class="ev ev--inf">computed from Table 9 and §7.4</span> <a class="goto" href="#tour" data-goto="2">open step 2 →</a> </p> </div> </section> <!-- 3 ------------------------------------------------------------------ --> <section id="s3"> <div class="sec-head"><span class="num">03</span><h2>What the frozen encoder is good for</h2></div> <div class="col"> <h3>The attentive probe, and what it measures</h3> <p data-ev="pap"> Four transformer blocks with 16 heads sit on the frozen encoder's tokens; the last block replaces self-attention with cross-attention from a single learnable query, and a linear classifier reads the query. <span class="ev ev--pap">Paper §5, App. C.1</span> </p> <p data-ev="inf"> A linear probe on pooled features measures what is linearly available in one vector, which undercounts a patch-token model with no class token. Full fine-tuning measures initialisation plus adaptation, and mixes the two. An attentive probe sits between: a small nonlinear reader that may attend anywhere in the token set while the encoder stays fixed. The four-block probe beats a single cross-attention block by 1.4 points on average for ViT-L and 1.0 for ViT-g. <span class="ev ev--pap">App. C.2</span> Two protocol details matter for comparisons. Each run trains 20 probe heads with different learning rates and weight decays and reports the best, with no separate validation split stated. Jester and Diving-48 read four encoder layers, not one. <span class="ev ev--pap">Table 15, Table 16</span> <a class="goto" href="#tour" data-goto="7">open step 7 →</a> </p> </div> <div class="panel"> class="app">85.3</td><td class="app">87.9</td><td class="app">83.8</td></tr> <tr><td>V-JEPA 2 ViT-g</td><td>1B</td><td>87.5</td><td class="mot">75.3</td><td class="mot">90.1</td><td class="mot">97.7</td><td class="app">86.6</td><td class="app">90.7</td><td class="app">84.6</td></tr> <tr class="best"><td>V-JEPA 2 ViT-g384 *</td><td>1B</td><td>88.2</td><td class="mot">77.3</td><td class="mot">90.2</td><td class="mot">97.8</td><td class="app">87.3</td><td class="app">91.1</td><td class="app">85.1</td></tr> </tbody> </table> </div> <div class="panel__foot" data-ev="pap">Blue columns need several frames (motion), orange ones can be solved from one (appearance). * The headline 77.3 uses 384 px and 64-frame SSv2 clips, a protocol no other row gets; at the shared protocol V-JEPA 2 scores 75.3. PE-core G reaches 89.8 on IN1K with its own probe at 448 px. <span class="ev ev--pap">Paper Table 4 and caption</span></div> </div> <div class="col"> <p data-ev="inf"> <b>Why the motion columns favour a JEPA.</b> The target of each masked tube is the feature of a region whose content is determined by the trajectory visible around it, so features that encode how things move are the cheapest way to predict it. Image-text contrastive encoders are trained per frame against captions, which name objects far more often than motions. The pattern in Table 4 fits: V-JEPA 2 leads SSv2 by 20 points over the best image encoder and trails SigLIP2 and PE on K400, COIN and ImageNet by 1 to 4. <span class="ev ev--inf">Inference</span> </p> <h3>Action anticipation (Epic-Kitchens-100)</h3> <p data-ev="pap"> The context clip ends 1 s before an action; the encoder reads 32 frames at 8 fps, the pretraining predictor is given mask tokens for the frame 1 s ahead, and a probe with three queries (action, verb, noun) trained with focal loss reads both. Recall@5 on actions: ViT-L 32.7, ViT-H 36.5, ViT-g 38.0, ViT-g384 39.7, against 27.6 for PlausiVL (8B), 26.0 for Video-LLaMA and 25.8 for InAViT, a 44% relative gain. <span class="ev ev--pap">Paper §6, Table 5, App. D.1</span> The probe-input ablation says what that gain is made of: encoder features alone 39.1, predictor output alone 20.2, both 39.7. Accuracy falls sharply as the anticipation time grows from 1 s to 10 s. <span class="ev ev--pap">Table 20, Fig. 18</span> The baselines are task-specific systems with different backbones and data; none is a frozen-encoder probe. <span class="ev ev--inf">Inference</span> </p> <h3>Video question answering</h3> <p data-ev="pap"> LLaVA-style alignment: encoder tokens, a projector, an LLM, trained in stages (captioning, image QA, video QA). The controlled comparison fixes Qwen2-7B-Instruct, 18M samples, a frozen encoder and 128 frames, and swaps the vision encoder: V-JEPA 2 ViT-g512 averages 52.3 against 49.1 for PE, 48.1 for SigLIP2 and 45.7 for DINOv2, with its largest margins on MVP, TemporalBench and TVBench, and a narrow loss on PerceptionTest (72.0 against 72.4). <span class="ev ev--pap">Paper §7.2, Table 6</span> The headline row uses Llama 3.1 8B, the full 88.5M samples, ViT-g384 with an MLP projector and 288 tokens per frame: PerceptionTest 84.0, MVP 44.5, TempCompass 76.9, TemporalBench 36.7, TOMATO 40.3, TVBench 60.6, MVBench 73.5, against PerceptionLM 8B's 82.7, 39.7, 72.7, 28.3, 33.2, 63.5 and 77.1. <span class="ev ev--pap">Table 8</span> </p> <div class="note" data-ev="inf"> <p><b>What the comparisons control.</b> The controlled table holds the LLM, data and stages fixed but not the token budget: V-JEPA 2 is pooled 4× to 8×, the image encoders 16×, and visual token counts range from 5,832 to 10,952 per video (Table 21). The headline table compares systems with different LLMs, alignment data and frame counts, and its PerceptionTest entry was fine-tuned on that benchmark while the rest are zero-shot. The defensible claim is the controlled one, and it is a claim about temporal benchmarks. <span class="ev ev--inf">Inference</span></p> </div> <h3>Physical-understanding probes</h3> <p data-ev="ctx"> On IntPhys 2 (violation of expectation, chance 50%), V-JEPA 2 ViT-H scores 54.0 easy, 58.5 medium, 59.4 hard, 57.5 overall and 56.4 held-out, against human majority votes of 96.2, 97.8, 95.5, 96.4 and 92.4. It is the best predictive model evaluated, and each model's figure is its best of about a dozen hyperparameter runs per column. On the original IntPhys, where V-JEPA ViT-H with RoPE reaches 98.3, V-JEPA 2 ViT-H reaches 87.2. <span class="ev ev--ctx">IntPhys 2, Table 2</span> </p> <p data-ev="inf"> The gap is not subtle. A frame-feature objective rewards continuity of appearance, which covers permanence and some continuity, but nothing in it penalises two solids passing through each other. MVP's 44.5 paired accuracy leads the 8B class, by 4.8 points over PerceptionLM, and is still far from a solved benchmark. <span class="ev ev--inf">Inference</span> </p> </div> </section> <!-- 4 ------------------------------------------------------------------ --> <section id="s4"> <div class="sec-head"><span class="num">04</span><h2>V-JEPA 2-AC: the action-conditioned world model</h2></div> <div class="panel"> <div class="panel__head">Post-training setup</div> <div class="panel__body"> <dl class="kv"> <dt>Encoder</dt><dd data-ev="pap">V-JEPA 2 ViT-g, frozen; each frame encoded alone, 16 × 16 × 1408. The code loads the EMA teacher weights for both context and targets. <span class="ev ev--pap">§3.1</span> <span class="ev ev--code">droid-256px-8f.yaml</span></dd> <dt>Predictor</dt><dd data-ev="pap">∼300M transformer, 24 layers, 16 heads, width 1024, GELU, block-causal. <span class="ev ev--pap">§3.1</span></dd> <dt>Data</dt><dd data-ev="pap">Raw DROID, left exocentric camera only, clips of 4 s at 4 fps, 256 × 256, videos under 4 s discarded: 23k trajectories, under 62 hours. Training on left and right views without conditioning on the camera degraded performance. <span class="ev ev--pap">§3.1, App. B.1</span></dd> <dt>State s<sub>k</sub></dt><dd data-ev="pap">7-D, relative to the robot base: x, y, z, three extrinsic Euler angles, gripper. <span class="ev ev--pap">§3.1</span></dd> <dt>Action a<sub>k</sub></dt><dd data-ev="code">s<sub>k+1</sub> ⊖ s<sub>k</sub>: Δxyz, the extrinsic Euler angles of R<sub>k+1</sub>R<sub>k</sub><sup>⊤</sup>, Δgripper. The repository's example step is Δ = (+9.2, +3.1, +8.4) cm, rotation (−0.017, −0.010, 0.000) rad, gripper 0. <span class="ev ev--code">droid.py poses_to_diffs; franka_example_traj.npz</span></dd> <dt>Rate</dt><dd data-ev="inf">4 fps nominal. The loader strides by ⌈f<sub>video</sub>/4⌉ frames, so for DROID's 15 fps recordings the effective rate is 3.75 Hz and one action spans about 0.27 s of teleoperation. <span class="ev ev--code">droid.py</span> <span class="ev ev--inf">Inference on the 15 fps source</span></dd> <dt>Optimisation</dt><dd data-ev="pap">AdamW, batch 256, weight decay 0.04; learning rate 7.5e-5 → 4.25e-4 over 4,500 iterations, constant for 85,500, decayed to 0 over 4,500. <span class="ev ev--pap">App. B.1</span></dd> </dl> </div> </div> <div class="col"> <h3>The predictor as a function</h3> <div class="formula">ẑ<sub>k+1</sub> = P<sub>φ</sub>( (a<sub>t</sub>, s<sub>t</sub>, z<sub>t</sub>)<sub>t ≤ k</sub> ),   z<sub>t</sub> = LN(E(x<sub>t</sub>)) ∈ ℝ<sup>256×1408</sup>,   a<sub>t</sub>, s<sub>t</sub> ∈ ℝ<sup>7</sup></div> <p data-ev="code"> Each time step contributes a block of 258 tokens in the order [a<sub>t</sub>, s<sub>t</sub>, 256 patches]. The attention mask lets every token in block t attend to every token in blocks 0 to t, including the action a<sub>t</sub> of its own block, and to nothing later. The patch outputs of block t are read as the prediction of frame t + 1. Patches carry the full 3D rotary position; action and state tokens get only the temporal rotation. <span class="ev ev--code">ac_predictor.py; modules.py build_action_block_causal_attention_mask</span> <span class="ev ev--pap">§3.1</span> </p> </div> <div class="panel"> <div class="panel__body"> <pre class="code">z (B, T·256, 1408) → linear → (B, T, 256, 1024) a, s (B, T, 7) → two separate linears → (B, T, 1, 1024) each <b>interleave</b> cat[a, s, z] per step → (B, T·258, 1024) <i># T = 16 → 4,128 tokens</i> <b>24 blocks</b> block-causal mask, (T·258)² entries, ≈53% allowed at T = 16 drop the 2 conditioning tokens per step → (B, T·256, 1024) → LN → linear → (B, T·256, 1408) <b>LN again</b> on the output, to match the LayerNormed targets</pre> </div> <div class="panel__foot" data-ev="code">From src/models/ac_predictor.py and app/vjepa_droid/train.py (normalize_reps: true). The allowed fraction is (T + 1)/(2T) of block pairs. <span class="ev ev--code">Code</span> <a class="goto" href="#tour" data-goto="9">open step 9 →</a></div> </div> <div class="col"> <h3>Teacher forcing plus a short rollout</h3> <div class="formula">L<sub>tf</sub> = (1/T) Σ<sub>k=1..T</sub> ‖P<sub>φ</sub>((a<sub>t</sub>, s<sub>t</sub>, z<sub>t</sub>)<sub>t≤k</sub>) − z<sub>k+1</sub>‖<sub>1</sub>,  T = 15     L<sub>roll</sub> = ‖P<sub>φ</sub>(a<sub>1:T</sub>; s<sub>1</sub>, z<sub>1</sub>) − z<sub>T+1</sub>‖<sub>1</sub>,  T = 2     L = L<sub>tf</sub> + L<sub>roll</sub></div> <p data-ev="pap"> Teacher forcing predicts every next frame from ground-truth latents. The rollout term feeds the first prediction back in and supervises the second, so the predictor is differentiated through one recurrent step. <span class="ev ev--pap">Eqs. 2 to 4</span> In code the rollout starts from [z<sub>0</sub>, ẑ<sub>1</sub>], appends ẑ<sub>2</sub>, and the loss is a per-element mean absolute error over both. <span class="ev ev--code">train.py, auto_steps: 2</span> </p> <p data-ev="inf"> Why so short: each rollout step reruns all 24 layers over a growing sequence and keeps the activations for the backward pass, and gradients through recurrent steps compound. What two steps buy is exposure to the model's own one-step error once, which controls the first-order drift term. Nothing constrains error at step five, which is one reason the planner never asks for step five. <span class="ev ev--inf">Inference</span> <a class="goto" href="#tour" data-goto="10">open step 10 →</a> </p> <h3>Planning as energy minimisation</h3> <div class="formula">E(â<sub>1:T</sub>; z<sub>k</sub>, s<sub>k</sub>, z<sub>g</sub>) = ‖ P(â<sub>1:T</sub>; s<sub>k</sub>, z<sub>k</sub>) − z<sub>g</sub> ‖<sub>1</sub>,   a<sup>⋆</sup><sub>1:T</sub> = argmin E,   execute a<sup>⋆</sup><sub>1</sub>, observe, repeat</div> <p data-ev="pap"> The current frame and the goal image are encoded by the same frozen encoder. The cross-entropy method samples each action coordinate from a Gaussian initialised at zero mean and unit variance, rolls every sample through the predictor, keeps the top k by energy, refits mean and variance to them, and after the last iteration returns the mean. Deployed settings: 800 samples, top 10 (an elite fraction of 1.25%), 10 iterations, horizon 1, blocking control that waits for each action to finish. <span class="ev ev--pap">§3.2, Fig. 7, §4.1, App. B.2</span> In the released planner the end-effector state during a rollout is not predicted: it is composed analytically from the previous state and the sampled action, rotations are sampled as zero, and the refit is smoothed with momentum. <span class="ev ev--code">notebooks/utils/mpc_utils.py</span> <a class="goto" href="#tour" data-goto="11">open step 11 →</a> </p> <p data-ev="pap"> The one energy landscape in the paper sweeps Δx and Δy over ±5 cm for a reach whose true displacement is (0, −10) cm. The minimum sits near (0, −5) cm, and the energy spans 0.40 to 0.44. <span class="ev ev--pap">Fig. 9</span> That minimum lies on the edge of the swept square: the figure shows the landscape slopes the right way, not that its minimum finds the true action, and the whole landscape varies by about 10% of its level. <span class="ev ev--inf">Inference</span> </p> </div> <div class="panel"> <div class="panel__head">Zero-shot deployment: success out of 10 trials per cell (Table 2)</div> <div class="panel__body tablewrap"> <table> <thead><tr><th scope="col">Method</th><th scope="col">Lab</th><th scope="col">Reach</th><th scope="col">Grasp cup</th><th scope="col">Grasp box</th><th scope="col">Reach w/ cup</th><th scope="col">Reach w/ box</th><th scope="col">P&P cup</th><th scope="col">P&P box</th></tr></thead> <tbody> <tr><td>Octo</td><td>1</td><td>100</td><td>20</td><td>0</td><td>20</td><td>70</td><td>20</td><td>10</td></tr> <tr><td>Octo</td><td>2</td><td>100</td><td>10</td><td>0</td><td>10</td><td>70</td><td>10</td><td>10</td></tr> <tr><td>V-JEPA 2-AC</td><td>1</td><td>100</td><td>70</td><td>30</td><td>90</td><td>80</td><td>80</td><td>80</td></tr> <tr class="best"><td>V-JEPA 2-AC</td><td>2</td><td>100</td><td>60</td><td>20</td><td>60</td><td>70</td><td>80</td><td>50</td></tr> <tr><td>Cosmos</td><td>2</td><td>80</td><td>0</td><td>20</td><td>n/a</td><td>n/a</td><td>0</td><td>0</td></tr> </tbody> </table> </div> <div class="panel__foot" data-ev="pap">Franka Panda arms with RobotiQ grippers in two labs absent from DROID, an uncalibrated low-resolution monocular RGB camera, operational-space control, the same weights and code in both labs. Cosmos row: Table 3, Lab 2, 80 samples, 10 iterations, horizon 1, 4 min per action; V-JEPA 2-AC there: 800 samples, 16 s per action; both on one RTX 4090. <span class="ev ev--pap">Paper §4, Tables 2 and 3</span></div> </div> <div class="col"> <p data-ev="pap"> <b>Tasks.</b> Reach moves the gripper to the pose in one goal image and ends within 4 cm in all three single-axis tests, with the distance falling at every step. Grasp and reach-with-object use one goal image. Pick-and-place is given two sub-goal images and the final goal: the planner chases the first (object grasped) for 4 steps, the second (object near the target) for 10, and the final for 4. <span class="ev ev--pap">§4.2, Fig. 8, App. B.2</span> <b>Baselines.</b> Octo starts from octo-base-1.5, trained on Open X-Embodiment, and is fine-tuned on all of DROID with hindsight image goals drawn up to 20 steps ahead, two frames of context and a 4-action horizon; it gets the better of blocking and non-blocking control. Cosmos starts from the 7B continuous-token latent diffusion model and is fine-tuned on DROID with three hand-made fixes (a lower learning rate, no conditioning dropout, noise raised by a factor of e²). <span class="ev ev--pap">§4.1</span> </p> <p data-ev="inf"> <b>Where the speed gap comes from.</b> One CEM evaluation of V-JEPA 2-AC at horizon 1 is one forward pass of a 300M predictor over 258 tokens, about 2 · 3×10⁸ · 258 ≈ 1.5×10¹¹ FLOPs. At 800 samples and 10 iterations that is 1.2×10¹⁵ FLOPs per action, which an RTX 4090 at half its roughly 165 TFLOP/s dense BF16 peak finishes in about 15 s, close to the reported 16 s. Cosmos spends 15× the time on a tenth of the samples, about 150× more per sample, because each sample is a multi-step denoising of video latents by a 7B network. V-JEPA 2.1's paper reports the same V-JEPA 2 planner at 3 s per action on an A100; neither paper reconciles the two timings. <span class="ev ev--inf">Inference</span> <span class="ev ev--ctx">V-JEPA 2.1 Table 6</span> <a class="goto" href="#tour" data-goto="12">open step 12 →</a> </p> <p data-ev="pap"> <b>Looking inside.</b> A deterministic ViT-L decoder trained with MSE to map V-JEPA 2 features back to pixels, applied off the shelf to predictor outputs, shows the arm animated and the background kept still; the cup ends slightly low after a rollout, and with an open gripper the cup stays put. <span class="ev ev--pap">App. B.3, Fig. 15</span> These are single qualitative examples. <span class="ev ev--inf">Inference</span> </p> <p data-ev="pap"> <b>Camera sensitivity, measured.</b> For camera positions from about 35° to 85° around the table, the robot makes 201 random x–y moves; for each, the planner infers the one-step action that best explains the next frame. A 2 × 2 least-squares map from inferred to executed actions has condition number ≈ 1.5, so it is close to a rotation; the rotation error grows almost linearly with camera angle, and the mean absolute error is about 1.6 cm on 5 cm moves. Rotating the planner's actions by that map would calibrate it without supervision; the experiments do not. <span class="ev ev--pap">App. B.4, Fig. 16</span> This is a controllability measurement, and it is the most informative quantitative diagnostic of the world model in the paper. <span class="ev ev--inf">Inference</span> </p> </div> </section> <!-- 5 ------------------------------------------------------------------ --> <section id="s5"> <div class="sec-head"><span class="num">05</span><h2>Evaluation audit and limitations</h2></div> <div class="col"> <h3>The robot numbers, with the intervals the paper leaves out</h3> <p data-ev="inf"> Every cell in Tables 2 and 3 is k successes in n = 10. The table below adds 95% Wilson intervals, computed by this page. At n = 10, 60% runs from 31% to 83%, and 60% against 80% is not a separable difference. Pooling both labs gives n = 20 per task for V-JEPA 2-AC and Octo, which is the fairest single number the data allow. <span class="ev ev--inf">computed here</span> </p> </div> <div class="panel"> <div class="panel__head"><span>Robot cells with Wilson 95% intervals</span><span class="readout" id="vj-ci-note" style="margin-left:auto"></span></div> <div class="panel__body tablewrap" id="vj-ci-table"></div> <div class="panel__foot" data-ev="pap">Counts from Tables 2 and 3. Trials varied object location and starting pose; per-condition outcomes are not reported. <span class="ev ev--pap">Paper §4.2</span></div> </div> <div class="col"> <h3>Protocol flags</h3> <ul class="checks"> <li data-ev="pap"><b>One model, no seeds.</b> Each method is one trained checkpoint; no training-seed variance is reported anywhere in §4. <span class="ev ev--pap">§4</span></li> <li data-ev="pap"><b>The camera was tuned for the method.</b> "We manually tried different camera positions before settling on one that worked well across all of our experiments." The baselines were run from the same placement. <span class="ev ev--pap">§4.3, App. B.4</span></li> <li data-ev="pap"><b>Goals are hand-made.</b> Start and goal images, and the sub-goal images for pick-and-place, were composed by the experimenters from the deployment camera. <span class="ev ev--pap">App. B.2, Fig. 14</span></li> <li data-ev="inf"><b>Baseline parity is partial.</b> Octo trains on all of DROID, several times the 23k trajectories, but only with goals at most 20 steps ahead, a different goal distribution from the tasks'. Cosmos gets a tenth of the samples because of its cost and runs in one lab only. The world models use blocking control; Octo gets its better mode. <span class="ev ev--inf">Inference from §4.1</span></li> <li data-ev="pap"><b>Same arm, same gripper class.</b> DROID is a Franka Panda with a Robotiq gripper, and so are both labs. Embodiment transfer is not tested. <span class="ev ev--pap">§4.1</span> <span class="ev ev--ctx">DROID</span></li> <li data-ev="ctx"><b>The successor's headline mixes settings.</b> V-JEPA 2.1's "+20 points on Grasp" is 60% for V-JEPA 2 at horizon 1 against 80% for V-JEPA 2.1 at horizon 8; at matched settings the difference is 60% against 70%, one trial in ten. <span class="ev ev--ctx">V-JEPA 2.1 Table 6</span></li> </ul> <h3>Which generalisation axes "zero-shot in new labs" actually tests</h3> </div> <div class="panel"> <div class="panel__body tablewrap"> <table> <thead><tr><th scope="col">Axis</th><th scope="col">Tested?</th><th scope="col">Evidence</th></tr></thead> <tbody> <tr><td>Environment (lab, lighting, background, clutter)</td><td class="lead">yes, 2 labs</td><td style="white-space:normal">Tables 2 and 3</td></tr> <tr><td>Object</td><td>weakly</td><td style="white-space:normal">two object types, a cup and a box, not in the training labs</td></tr> <tr><td>Spatial (object location, start pose)</td><td>varied, not reported</td><td style="white-space:normal">"various permutations to the task across trials"</td></tr> <tr><td>Instruction</td><td>not applicable</td><td style="white-space:normal">goals are images; language goals are future work (§4.3)</td></tr> <tr><td>Camera viewpoint</td><td>measured offline only</td><td style="white-space:normal">App. B.4 shows sensitivity; task success at other views is not reported</td></tr> <tr><td>Embodiment</td><td>no</td><td style="white-space:normal">same arm and gripper class as DROID</td></tr> <tr><td>Task horizon</td><td>no</td><td style="white-space:normal">sub-goal images supplied for the only multi-stage task</td></tr> </tbody> </table> </div> </div> <div class="col"> <h3>Four axes for a world model, reported separately</h3> </div> <div class="panel"> <div class="panel__body tablewrap"> <table> <thead><tr><th scope="col">Axis</th><th scope="col">What V-JEPA 2-AC reports</th><th scope="col">Protocol that would settle it</th></tr></thead> <tbody> <tr><td>Latent fidelity</td><td style="white-space:normal">decoded frames for one trajectory (Fig. 15); no error curves</td><td style="white-space:normal">open-loop k-step ‖ẑ − z‖, normalised by ‖z<sub>t+k</sub> − z<sub>t</sub>‖ so copy-last-frame scores 1, plus a pose probe on ẑ</td></tr> <tr><td>Controllability</td><td style="white-space:normal">inferred-action axis vs camera angle (App. B.4); open vs closed gripper, qualitative</td><td style="white-space:normal">action-sensitivity ratio; an inverse-dynamics probe on predicted states</td></tr> <tr><td>Planning quality</td><td style="white-space:normal">one 2-D energy slice (Fig. 9)</td><td style="white-space:normal">rank correlation between planner energy and realised distance to goal over executed plans</td></tr> <tr><td>Task success</td><td style="white-space:normal">Tables 2 and 3, n = 10 per cell</td><td style="white-space:normal">3+ seeds, ≥ 50 episodes per task, Wilson or bootstrap intervals</td></tr> </tbody> </table> </div> <div class="panel__foot" data-ev="inf">The normalisation in row one is the minimum fix for latent error being measured in a space the model chose; the pose probe fixes the target so that encoders can be compared. <span class="ev ev--inf">Inference</span></div> </div> <div class="col"> <h3>Why a low latent error certifies nothing</h3> <div id="vj-reasons"></div> <h3>Limitations, stated and inferred</h3> <ul class="checks"> <li data-ev="pap"><b>Camera position.</b> The action frame is inferred from the image, often without the robot base in view, so the model's axes rotate with the camera. <span class="ev ev--pap">§4.3, App. B.4</span></li> <li data-ev="pap"><b>Long horizons.</b> Autoregressive error grows and the search space grows exponentially with horizon; pick-and-place needs sub-goal images. <span class="ev ev--pap">§4.3</span></li> <li data-ev="pap"><b>Image goals only.</b> Language goals are named as future work. <span class="ev ev--pap">§4.3, §9</span></li> <li data-ev="inf"><b>A non-convex energy with no uncertainty.</b> CEM with 10 elites can lock onto a wrong basin, and a deterministic predictor offers no signal for when its prediction is off-manifold. <span class="ev ev--inf">Inference</span></li> <li data-ev="inf"><b>Slow, blocking, gripper-only control.</b> About 4 Hz at best, 16 s of planning per step, a parallel gripper, rigid objects. Contact transients, deformables and dexterous hands are outside what was tested. <span class="ev ev--inf">Inference</span></li> </ul> <div class="note note--warn" data-ev="inf"> <p><b>The failure mode worth naming: close in embedding, wrong in the world.</b> The energy is a uniform mean over 256 × 1408 numbers. A cup covers a handful of the 256 patches, so a state where the gripper stands in the right place with the cup beside it rather than between the fingers differs from the goal in perhaps 4 patches, about 1.6% of the terms, which is small next to the 10% dynamic range in Fig. 9. The gripper occluding the cup looks like a grasp; a cup balanced on a finger and a cup held look alike in one frame. The planner is rewarded for matching pixels' features, and physics is only present to the extent those features encode it. V-JEPA 2.1's own failure analysis points the same way: its pick-and-place and grasp failures are gripper timing, closing too early or opening in transit. <span class="ev ev--inf">Inference</span> <span class="ev ev--ctx">V-JEPA 2.1 §3.3</span> <a class="goto" href="#tour" data-goto="13">open step 13 →</a></p> </div> </div> </section> <!-- 6 ------------------------------------------------------------------ --> <section id="s6"> <div class="sec-head"><span class="num">06</span><h2>Positioning, cost, and the missing experiments</h2></div> <div class="panel"> <div class="panel__head">What each system predicts and how it acts</div> <div class="panel__body tablewrap"> <table> <thead><tr><th scope="col">System</th><th scope="col">Predicts</th><th scope="col">Conditions on</th><th scope="col">Acts by</th><th scope="col">Role</th></tr></thead> <tbody> <tr class="best"><td>V-JEPA 2-AC</td><td>next-frame latents</td><td style="white-space:normal">frame latents, 7-D action and state</td><td style="white-space:normal">CEM over its own rollouts, MPC</td><td>simulator for a planner</td></tr> <tr><td>Cosmos 3 (forward dynamics)</td><td>video latents, decodable</td><td style="white-space:normal">video, text, a unified action interface</td><td style="white-space:normal">policy mode denoises actions and video together</td><td>renderer, simulator, policy</td></tr> <tr><td>Genie 3</td><td>pixels, real time</td><td style="white-space:normal">navigation controls, text events</td><td style="white-space:normal">an agent or person drives it</td><td>renderer, interactive simulator</td></tr> <tr><td>World Labs Atlas</td><td>pixels, geometry-grounded</td><td style="white-space:normal">text, images, camera pose, depth; no action</td><td>n/a</td><td>renderer</td></tr> <tr><td>DreamerV3</td><td>latents, with a decoder</td><td style="white-space:normal">actions; trained with rewards per environment</td><td style="white-space:normal">actor-critic learned in imagination</td><td>simulator inside an RL learner</td></tr> <tr><td>TD-MPC2</td><td>latents, decoder-free</td><td style="white-space:normal">actions; reward and value heads</td><td style="white-space:normal">MPPI with a learned policy prior and terminal value</td><td>simulator plus planner</td></tr> <tr><td>DINO-WM, LeWM</td><td>latents</td><td style="white-space:normal">actions; per-environment offline data</td><td style="white-space:normal">CEM, MPC</td><td>simulator for a planner</td></tr> <tr><td>π0 / π0.5, OpenVLA</td><td>actions</td><td style="white-space:normal">images, language, proprioception</td><td style="white-space:normal">direct action chunks (π0) or tokens (OpenVLA)</td><td>policy</td></tr> </tbody> </table> </div> <div class="panel__foot" data-ev="ctx">Sources: each system's own paper or announcement; Cosmos 3 and Atlas from my earlier teardowns on this site. Step 15 places them on renderer, simulator and planner axes. <span class="ev ev--ctx">Context</span> <a class="goto" href="#tour" data-goto="15">open step 15 →</a></div> </div> <div class="col"> <h3>What it costs</h3> <p data-ev="inf"> <b>Pretraining.</b> GPU-hours are not disclosed as a number. The paper puts the full-resolution counterfactual at about 60 GPU-years and the progressive schedule at 8.4× less, which implies about 7 A100-years, some 62,000 GPU-hours, for the 64-frame 384 px ViT-g. <span class="ev ev--inf">derived from §2.4</span> The released configs run pretraining on 16 nodes of 8 GPUs at 24 clips each and the cooldown on 64 nodes at 6 clips each. <span class="ev ev--code">configs/train/vitg16</span> </p> <p data-ev="inf"> <b>Post-training.</b> Not disclosed; the released config uses 4 nodes of 8 GPUs. Counting a frozen ViT-g forward pass on 16 frames (about 8×10¹² FLOPs) and a 300M predictor trained on 4,128 tokens with a two-step rollout (about 1.1×10¹³), the 94.5k-iteration run at batch 256 comes to roughly 5×10²⁰ FLOPs, on the order of 1,000 A100-hours at 40% utilisation. Caching encoder features removes the first term after one pass. <span class="ev ev--inf">Inference</span> </p> <p data-ev="pap"> <b>Inference.</b> 16 s per action on one RTX 4090. A pick-and-place episode of 4 + 10 + 4 = 18 planning steps therefore spends about 4.8 minutes planning; the same episode with Cosmos at 4 minutes per step takes 72 minutes, the "over one hour" the paper reports. <span class="ev ev--pap">§4.2, Table 3</span> </p> <h3>Three experiments a strong reviewer would demand</h3> <ol class="checks" style="list-style:decimal;padding-left:1.2rem"> <li style="padding-left:0" data-ev="spec"><b>Encoder ablation at the AC stage.</b> The same predictor and 62 hours over V-JEPA 2, DINOv2, SigLIP2 and V-JEPA 2.1, each frozen, plus V-JEPA 2 fine-tuned, three seeds each, evaluated on Reach, Grasp and pick-and-place with 50 trials per cell. This is the direct test of the paper's central bet. Cost: 15 runs at about 1,000 A100-hours is 15,000 GPU-hours, far less with cached features, plus about 30 robot-days. <span class="ev ev--spec">Speculation</span></li> <li style="padding-left:0" data-ev="spec"><b>Rollout-loss horizon sweep.</b> Train with rollout horizons 1, 2, 4 and 8; report open-loop latent error at 1 to 16 steps and closed-loop success at planning horizons 1, 4 and 8. V-JEPA 2.1 found longer planning horizons hurt V-JEPA 2 and helped 2.1, so the interaction is real. Cost: 4 runs, 1,000 to 3,000 A100-hours each as rollout memory grows. <span class="ev ev--spec">Speculation</span> <span class="ev ev--ctx">V-JEPA 2.1 §3.3</span></li> <li style="padding-left:0" data-ev="spec"><b>Camera and goal robustness.</b> Five camera positions from 35° to 85°, with and without the App. B.4 rotation calibration, and goal images taken from a different episode; 50 trials per cell. No training required: about 40 hours of planning compute and robot time for 500 episodes. <span class="ev ev--spec">Speculation</span></li> </ol> </div> </section> <!-- 7 ------------------------------------------------------------------ --> <section id="s7"> <div class="sec-head"><span class="num">07</span><h2>Concept guide</h2></div> <div class="col"><p>Fourteen prerequisites, each with a mechanical picture and one line of maths. Click a card for the picture.</p></div> <div class="gloss" id="vj-gloss"></div> </section> <!-- 8 ------------------------------------------------------------------ --> <section id="tour"> <div class="sec-head"><span class="num">08</span><h2>Interactive tour: one mechanism per step</h2></div> <div class="col"> <p> Sixteen steps, each isolating one mechanism, then a summary. Drag a 3D scene to orbit it; click it and scroll, or use + and −, to zoom. The segment bar under each scene highlights, isolates, hides or explodes a component, and hovering a component names it with its tensor shape. The depth switch changes how much each caption says, never the scene. Arrow keys move between steps when the tour has focus. The colour code is the same in every scene. </p> </div> <div class="tour" id="vj-tour" data-depth="2" tabindex="-1"> <div class="tour__bar"> <button type="button" class="btn" id="vj-prev"
